An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate

Acknowledgements
Public archives

The Association for the History of Nature Protection thanks the public archives mission
of the Ministry of Ecological Transition and, in particular, the members of the team
handling heritage-interest fonds in the central administration, who handled the
preparation, description and transfer of the fonds to the National Archives, under the
responsibility of the mission heads.

It also thanks the archives mission of the Ministry of Agriculture as well as the network
of archivists in the decentralized services (DDE, DDTM, DEAL,
DREAL1) and of our ministries' operators (AFFSET, ADEME, ANDRA,
IFREMER) who contributed to defining and implementing an archiving
policy and to processing and depositing heritage-character files
with the National or Departmental Archives.

This heritage conservation and its promotion could not have been achieved
without the commitment of the successive archives project officers and heritage
curators: Jean Luquet, Vincent Maroteaux,
Sylvie Desachy, Juliette Nunez, Bernard Vuillet, Françoise Lemaire, Christèle Noulet
and Camille Tatger, Hélène Zettel.
I would like to thank Mounia Megharbi in particular for writing the historical
introductions of the services and the ministers' biographies.

Thanks to this synergy, the central administration services of the ministry have deposited
more than 30,000 files and 7 TB of electronic archives with the National Archives. The
decentralized services have deposited more than 8,000 files with the Departmental Archives.

1 A list of acronyms is available in the appendix
AHPNE                                            20/04/26                            page 8/523
    An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate

Private archives — If we set aside today's Société nationale de protection de la nature (SNPN), which is
the heir of the Imperial Zoological Acclimatization Society founded in 1854, the
Society for the Protection of Landscapes and the Aesthetics of France (SPPEF),
founded in 1901, and the French League for the Protection of Birds (LPO) founded in
1912, most of the nature and environment protection
associations appeared from the late 1950s in France, such as the Society for the Study and Protection of Nature in
Brittany (SEPNB) or the Association for the Prevention of
Air Pollution (APPA) created in 1968. It was above all in the two decades
that followed that they multiplied across the different French regions.
Some associations are generalist and work in all areas of
environmental protection, at the regional or departmental level. Others are more
specialized and concentrate on a territory and/or a specific issue. Their modes of
action are many: their activists inform citizens, meet elected officials and decision-makers, carry out field actions (site clean-ups, naturalist
inventories...), train volunteers, manage protected areas, or take part in public debates and consultations.

The safeguarding and promotion of the archives of private actors in nature and
environment protection — with a view, in particular, to their use by
historians and researchers — is one of the motivations of the Association for the History of Nature
and Environment Protection (AHPNE). Besides the public
archives whose conservation is ensured by the National and
Departmental Archives, there exists a multitude of documents and environmental archive fonds held by individuals and
associations that are neither classified nor inventoried and are therefore generally not known and, as a result, not readily
available.
Consequently, the AHPNE has set itself, among other objectives, that of promoting the gathering, classification and conservation of every document useful to the knowledge of this history.

AHPNE                                      20/04/26                                page 9/523
        An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate

Foreword

Public archives
In accordance with articles 213-1 to 3 of the Heritage Code, which governs the time limits for public access to documents, and in respect of it, the archivists of
the archives mission within the Ministry of Ecological and Solidarity Transition work
to preserve the written record of the territory's natural heritage. Likewise, the Departmental
Archives control and collect the production of archives emanating from the State's
decentralized services.


---


Private archives
If we set aside today's Société nationale de protection de la nature (SNPN), which is
the heir of the Imperial Zoological Acclimatization Society founded in 1854, the Society
for the Protection of Landscapes and the Aesthetics of France (SPPEF), founded in 1901,
and the League for the Protection of Birds (LPO) founded in 1912, most of the nature and
environment protection associations appeared from the late 1950s in France, such as the
Society for the Study and Protection of Nature in Brittany (SEPNB) or the Association
for the Prevention of Air Pollution (APPA) created in 1968. It was above all in the two
decades that followed that they multiplied across the different French regions.
Some associations are generalist and work in all areas of environmental protection, at
the regional or departmental level. Others are more specialized and concentrate on a
territory and/or a specific issue. Their modes of action are many: their activists inform
citizens, meet elected officials and decision-makers, carry out field actions (site
clean-ups, naturalist inventories...), train volunteers, manage protected areas, or take
part in public debates and consultations.

The safeguarding and promotion of the archives of private actors in nature and environment
protection — with a view, in particular, to their use by historians and researchers — is
one of the motivations of the Association for the History of Nature and Environment
Protection (AHPNE). Besides the public archives whose conservation is ensured by the
National and Departmental Archives, there exists a multitude of documents and
environmental archive fonds held by individuals and associations that are neither
classified nor inventoried and are therefore generally not known and, as a result, not
readily available.
Consequently, the AHPNE has set itself, among other objectives, that of promoting the
gathering, classification and conservation of every document useful to the knowledge of
this history.

AHPNE                                     18/04/26                                page 10/523


---


An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate — Following a survey conducted in 2010 among the member associations of the France
Nature Environnement network, the AHPNE observed the following situation:
    •   The existence of several thousand nature protection associations across the
        territory
    •   The low level of classification, conservation (in good conditions) and
        promotion of association archives
    •   The few deposits of these archives to the National and Departmental Archives
    •   The difficulty of access to these archives due to ignorance of their existence
For associations, a double question arises regarding their archive fonds:
that of their physical conservation at the time of generational turnover among
officers and staff and in the course of relocations, and that of the transmission of
a collective memory to their members, their staff and their administrators — something
that is often lacking.
Alongside this finding, the AHPNE had noted the context of a low number of
research projects, works and university teaching in the field of environmental
protection in France, and therefore little use of archival sources.

To answer these two needs, the AHPNE embarked on writing archive
inventories, first on an experimental basis in the departments of Finistère and
Seine-Maritime, then, in 2018, by producing the inventory for the six departments of the
Centre-Val de Loire region2

The AHPNE has produced these archive inventories since 2011 in partnership with the
National Archives, the Departmental Archives of Finistère, and of the Centre-Val de-Loire
Region (Cher, Eure-et-Loir, Indre, Indre-et-Loire, Loir-et-Cher, Loiret) and the
associations concerned.
The departmental archives of Seine-Maritime produced the inventory of the department's
associations themselves.
The inventories could be produced thanks to the funding
- of the Ministry of Ecological and Solidarity Transition (Directorate of Water and
Biodiversity) for Finistère,
- of the Centre-Val de Loire Regional Council and of France Nature Environnement for that
region.
This guide to sources has the merit of presenting, for the first time, a synthesis of the
public and private archive fonds deposited with the National and Departmental Archives.
It presents many points of interest, both for the memory of the central administration
services and the decentralized services of our ministries, but it will also be a
precious tool useful to our fellow citizens and associations interested in this theme.
2   The full set of inventories is available on the websites
    - https://ressources.histoire-environnement.org/Ressources
    - http://www.archives.developpement-durable.gouv.fr/

AHPNE                                              18/04/26                         page 11/523

An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate — This guide is at once a tool serving a memory for action, because an amnesic
society cannot properly evolve; it also constitutes a memory for history, too often
threatened by neglect, or conversely by the appropriation of documents that then
escape the collective heritage as well.

Such are the stakes of this guide.

Hélène Zettel                                                               Patrick Février

AHPNE                                     18/04/26                                page 12/523
         An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate

Introduction

For the 1st time, a research tool presents sources from historical public
archives but also private ones.

Public archives

Panorama of the definitive archives

The guide presents the catalogue of the definitive archives (or archives of historical
interest) deriving from the current ministries and their predecessors. As mentioned
previously, these archives have been deposited with the heritage archive services where
they are conserved: National Archives (central administration files) or departmental
archives (decentralized services).

Chronological and administrative scope3

This guide therefore covers nearly forty-six years of inventories of ministerial
heritage archives, in connection with the energy and climate theme.

In parallel, decentralized services are being created at regional and departmental level.

At regional level, the Regional Directorates of the Environment, Planning and Housing
(DREAL) resulted from the merger of the Regional Directorates of the Environment
(DIREN), the Regional Directorates of Public Works (DRE) and the Regional Directorates
of Industry, Research and the Environment (DRIRE). They operate in the fields of water,
biodiversity, risk forecasting and prevention, energy, planning, urbanism and
construction, transport mobility and its infrastructure, as well as housing. In the
overseas territories, the Directorates of the Environment, Planning and Housing (DEAL)
correspond to metropolitan DREALs with in addition the remits of the departmental
directorates covering the ministry's scope, as well as the Maritime Inter-Regional
Directorates (DIRM) created in metropolitan France.

At departmental level, outside the overseas territories, the Departmental Directorates
of Territories – and of the Sea – (DDT(M)) result from the merger of the Departmental
Directorates of Public Works (DDE), the Departmental Directorates of Agriculture and
Forestry (DDAF) and, for coastal departments, the Departmental Directorates of Maritime
Affairs (DDAM).

3   In this volume you will find a few archive fonds covering the 19th-20th centuries. The
    references come from Andrée Covol's work "Les sources de l'histoire de l'environnement", 2003

An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate

This guide presents a few archive fonds of decentralized services deposited with the
Departmental Archives.

Through this guide to sources, you will find a century of archives of the ministerial
offices, illustrating the unfolding of public policies in the field of energy and
climate.

The impetus for these public policies came through the ministerial offices, then through
the operational directorates of the central administrations, and finally was rolled out
locally by the ministry's regional or departmental offices (departmental directorates,
regional directorates...).

Thus, the aim is to inventory exhaustively:

. the government archives entrusted by the members of the ministerial offices;

. the archives of the central administrations in charge of the energy and climate
domain

Only a few heritage archive fonds of the decentralized services are listed here.

Material importance of the files

Inventories (répertoires)

For each detailed inventory are mentioned the originating service, the extreme dates,
the detailed description, the reference code, location, access conditions. A dynamic
link allows direct access to the "Historical Archives" websites of the ministry4, the
virtual inventory room of the National Archives5, and the websites of the Departmental
Archives6.

This guide brings together more than 300,000 described files. A few posters. 9 GB of
digital archives.

AHPNE

An overview of public and private archive holdings concerning energy and climate

Private archives
The Association for the History of Nature and Environment Protection (AHPNE)
has set itself the objective of promoting the gathering, classification and conservation
of every document useful to the knowledge of this history.
Following a 2010 survey of associations, the AHPNE observed the following
situation:
    •   Several thousand nature protection associations across the territory
    •   Little research in the field of environmental protection in France
    •   Few donations to the National/Departmental Archives
    •   No conservation or promotion of archives within the associations?

To answer these questions, the AHPNE progressively set up a policy of
writing archive inventories7

The AHPNE has therefore produced these archive inventories since 2011 in partnership
with the National Archives, the Departmental Archives of Finistère, and of the
Centre-Val de-Loire Region (Cher, Eure-et-Loir, Indre, Indre-et-Loire, Loir-et-Cher,
Loiret). The departmental archives of Seine-Maritime produced those concerning their
own department themselves.

The inventories could be produced thanks to the funding
    – of the Ministry of Ecological and Solidarity Transition for Finistère,
    – of the Centre-Val de Loire Regional Council for that region.
    – of France Nature Environnement

This guide brings together more than xxx descriptions of the archive fonds of private
actors or nature protection associations conserved at the National/Departmental
Archives or in their own premises (more than xxx files, xxx TB of digital archives)
                                                               Patrick Février, President of the AHPNE

Michel d'Ornano, Minister of the Environment and Quality of Life, 1978-1981

After the legislative elections of March 1978, Michel d'Ornano, deputy for Calvados, was
appointed under the presidency of Valéry Giscard d'Estaing to head the new Ministry of
the Environment and Quality of Life. This innovative administration, which had to make
the "developers" and the "protectors" cohabit, received new remits previously
assigned to the Ministry of Public Works and Spatial Planning. The
historic monuments and architecture services, until then attached to the
Ministry of Culture, also joined this new structure. Intended to
unite Public Works and the Environment within a single ministry, this ministerial office ended
on 13 May 1981. It would take until 2007 to see the Public Works services
merge again with those of the Environment within the Ministry of Ecology,
Development and Spatial Planning.

Aiming at a modern conception of territorial management in its various aspects
(the environment, quality of life, housing), Michel d'Ornano worked on a
reconfiguration of ministerial structures. He launched the project of transforming the
territorial subdivisions into local public works and environment
agencies (ALEE), whose purpose was to bring environmental
concerns down to the ministry's territorial structures and to generalize the decentralization of
administration in matters of urbanism and housing. This project never went beyond the
experimental stage, the political and governmental alternation of May 1981 bringing to an end the
government from which it had sprung.11

Deposit 1979065812

Ministry: Environment and Quality of Life
Directorate: Ministerial Office (Cabinet)
Sub-directorate: Office of the Cabinet

Contents

on energy policy, spatial planning strategy and the
upgrading of the overseas departments and territories (DOM-TOM), the fight against
inflation and the new orientation of housing assistance
1975-1978

Laurent Fabius, Minister of Industry and Research, 1983-1984 — Born 20 August 1946 in Paris, Laurent Fabius is a French politician, member of the
Socialist Party.

A former student of the École normale supérieure, holder of the agrégation in modern
letters, and a former student of the Paris Institute of Political Studies, Mr Laurent
Fabius became an auditor at the Council of State in 1973, then master of requests in
1981 upon leaving the National School of Administration
(class of François Rabelais).

A member of the Socialist Party since 1974, Laurent Fabius became spokesman of
François Mitterrand, then First Secretary of the Socialist Party.

He was elected deputy for the 4th constituency of Seine-Maritime in 1968. He has been
re-elected to this seat eight times in a row since his first election.

In 1981, following François Mitterrand's victory in the presidential election,
Laurent Fabius was appointed minister delegate for the Budget.

From March 1983, he was appointed Minister of Industry and Research.

In April of that year, President Mitterrand announced the creation of a grand ministry
of modernization — combining with the Ministry of Industry and Research the
missions of industrial modernization and industrial redeployment — which "gathers in
his hands, under the authority of the Prime Minister, exceptional powers that will
enable him to implement the restructuring plan in all its aspects". The
post was all the more important since, at the time, the largest industrial
companies were within the State's fold. Two former members of Fabius's office,
Louis Schweitzer and Serge Weinberg, later went on to careers in the business world.

At 37, he became Prime Minister in 1983, and it was Édith Cresson who succeeded him as
Minister of Industrial Redeployment and Foreign Trade.

He became President of the National Assembly in 1988. In parallel, he held
numerous local mandates, such as mayor of Grand-Quevilly from 1995 to 2000 and
president of the Rouen agglomeration from 1989 to 1995. He was appointed Minister of
Foreign Affairs in May 2012.

Since 8 March 2016, he has been President of the Constitutional Council.

Edmond Hervé, Jean Auroux, Secretaries of State under the Minister of
Industry and Research, 1983-1984

Edmond Hervé
Edmond Hervé, born 3 December 1942 in La Bouillie (Côtes-du-Nord, now Côtes-d'Armor),
is a French politician, member of the Socialist Party, mayor of Rennes from 1977 to
2008 and senator from 2008 to 2014 for the department of Ille-et-Vilaine.

The son of tenant farmers, he holds a degree in public law from the University of
Rennes and also holds a postgraduate diploma in public law and another in political
science.

He became mayor of Rennes in 1977. Two years later, he gave up his
teaching profession to devote himself exclusively to his mandate.

As President of the District of Rennes, which later became the Rennes Métropole
agglomeration community, he pursued an intensive urban policy: social diversity,
support for culture, ecology, pedestrianized areas, development of public transport
(notably with the VAL-type automated metro) and of research linked to new
technologies (the Rennes Atalante technopole).

He held numerous ministerial posts under François Mitterrand's first term in office, including:
  - 22 May – 22 June 1981: Minister of Health in Pierre Mauroy's government (1).
 - 23 June 1981 – 29 June 1982: minister delegate to the Minister of Industry, in charge
of energy, in Pierre Mauroy's government (2).
  - 29 June 1982 – 22 March 1983: minister delegate to the Minister of State, Minister of
Research and Industry, in charge of energy, in Pierre Mauroy's government (2).

It was Jean Auroux who succeeded him in this ministerial post.

Martin Malvy, Secretary of State under the Minister of Industrial Redeployment
and Foreign Trade, in charge of energy, 1984-1986

Martin Malvy, born 24 February 1936 in Paris's 16th arrondissement, is a
journalist, French politician and former minister of the French government.
He is the grandson of Louis Malvy, Minister of the Interior during the First World War,
and the great-grandson of Charles de Verninac, a senator.

Martin Malvy worked as a journalist in the local press between 1973 and
1977, before entering the political arena under the colours of the Socialist Party.
First a departmental councillor and mayor of Figeac, he was elected deputy in 1968 and
remained so until 1998, apart from the three periods when he was part of the Fabius and
then Bérégovoy governments.

Under the presidency of François Mitterrand, he was appointed in the
government of Laurent Fabius as Secretary of State under the Minister of Industrial
Redeployment and Foreign Trade, in charge of energy (24 July 1984-20 March 1986). Louis
Mermaz succeeded him in this post.

Socialist President of the Midi-Pyrénées region from 15 March 1998 to 31 December 2015.
In 2010, he achieved the highest score in the French regional elections. From 1 to 4
January 2016, he was interim president of the Languedoc-Roussillon-Midi-Pyrénées
regional council.

Martin Malvy was also president of the Midi-Pyrénées regional council from 1998 to 2015.
In January 2012, he was appointed to the Supervisory Commission of the Caisse des Dépôts
et Consignations.

Alain Madelin, Minister of Industry, Posts and Telecommunications, and
Tourism, 1986-1988
Alain Madelin, born 26 March 1946 in Paris's 12th arrondissement, is a French
politician. He was minister three times between 1986 and 1995 and ran under the
Démocratie libérale label in the 2002 presidential election, winning 3.91% of
the votes.

The son of Gaétan Madelin, a specialized worker at Renault who went on to hold several
jobs, and of Aline Madelin, a typist-secretary, he spent his childhood in the
Belleville district of Paris.

After leaving school in the fourth year of secondary school, he took a vocational
aptitude certificate from 1963 to 1963 [sic], then a professional certificate as a fitter,
turner and miller at the Lycée Voltaire.

He obtained a law degree and was sworn in as a lawyer in 1973. He then
worked in various employers' institutes and organizations, notably with Georges Albertini.

In the autumn of 1968, he joined the National Federation of Independent Republicans
(FNRI) of Valéry Giscard d'Estaing. During the 1974 presidential campaign, he was
publisher of an anti-socialist newspaper, Spécial Banlieue, and joined Valéry
Giscard d'Estaing's campaign staff, which won the election. In the 1981 election, he
was again part of Giscard's campaign team.

Closely tied to the collective of the new economists, which aimed to promote the
Austrian school of economics in France and to rediscover French liberal thinkers, Alain
Madelin was, in the 1980s, one of the promoters of economic liberalism, which
was then gaining influence within the French right-wing parties.

Minister of Industry, Posts and Telecommunications and Tourism (1986-1988)
When the right won the legislative elections in 1986 and the first
cohabitation began, Jacques Chirac appointed him Minister of Industry, Posts and
Telecommunications and Tourism. The scenario repeated itself when the right won
the 1993 legislative elections and Édouard Balladur, prime minister of the second
cohabitation, appointed Alain Madelin Minister of Enterprises and Economic
Development.

His time in office left two notable marks: on the one hand, the "Madelin" retirement
contracts, allowing non-salaried workers to build up a pension through capitalization;
on the other, a simplification of business-creation procedures, with the development of
the single-person company status (EURL and EARL).

Succeeding him in this ministry, split into three, were Paul Quilès for telecommunications,
Roger Fauroux for industry and François Doubin for tourism.

In October 1994, he launched his own political movement, Idées Action, which brought
together economic decision-makers as well as right-wing elected officials and activists.
"Madelinism" then federated around this structure, which diagnosed a breakdown of the
social elevator and advocated making the State more competitive by lowering
compulsory levies.

Idées Action, which was more of a think tank than a political party, claimed
7,000 members in the late 1990s. Nicknamed by its founder the "house of
liberals", the movement allowed Alain Madelin to organize his networks and mobilize
his supporters.

In 2002, Alain Madelin ran in the presidential election, winning only
3.91% of the votes. In 2007, he withdrew from political life, turning to the fields of
industry and finance. In 2011, he co-founded Latour Capital, a venture capital
fund which later bought four subsidiaries of Véolia.

He also chairs the Public Interest Group for Digital Education in Africa.

Alain Carignon, Minister of the Environment, 1986-1988
Alain Carignon was the former Rally for the Republic (RPR) mayor of
Grenoble from 1983 to 1995.

He was minister delegate for the environment in the Chirac II government, then Minister
of Communication in the Balladur government from 1993 until his resignation the
following year.

In 1986, he won the legislative elections in his city's constituency and was called by
Jacques Chirac into the first cohabitation government, as minister delegate for the
environment. On 11 May 1986, shortly after the Chernobyl disaster, he declared on
Antenne 2's television news:
  "The highest radioactivity levels in France are below the thresholds beyond
which there is danger, and well below: 5, 10 or 100 times below.",
repeating the information communicated to the government by the central service
for protection against ionizing radiation.

According to a 2016 report by the Institute for Radiological Protection and Nuclear
Safety, the levels were in reality only 4 times below the precaution threshold set by
the World Health Organization, which was 2,000 Bq per litre, and 20 times below the
danger threshold set at 10,000 Bq per litre (except in the special case of Corsica).

Following this disaster, he proposed, in collaboration with the Ministry of the Interior,
a law to Parliament on the prevention of major risks. It was adopted
unanimously in 1987.

Dominique Strauss-Kahn, Minister of Industry and Foreign
Trade, 1991-1993
Dominique Strauss-Kahn, born 25 April 1949 in Neuilly-sur-Seine, is an economist,
French politician and international senior civil servant.

He was Socialist deputy for Haute-Savoie then for Val-d'Oise from 1986, chair of
the Finance Committee of the National Assembly from 1988 to 1991, Minister of
Industry and Foreign Trade from 1991 to 1993, in the Édith Cresson and Pierre
Bérégovoy governments, and mayor of Sarcelles from 1995 to 1997.

After contributing to the plural left's victory in the 1997 legislative elections,
he was appointed Minister of the Economy, Finance and Industry in the Lionel
Jospin government. Implicated in legal cases in 1999, he resigned from the
government, then regained his seat as deputy in 2001, before benefiting from a
dismissal and an acquittal. A candidate in the 2006 Socialist primary, he was beaten
in the first round by Ségolène Royal.

On the proposal of French President Nicolas Sarkozy, he became managing director
of the International Monetary Fund (IMF) in 2007. At the head of the institution, he
was notably confronted with the 2008 global banking and financial crisis and the
beginnings of the debt crisis in the euro zone.

Michel Barnier, Minister of the Environment, 1993-1995

A project officer in Robert Poujade's office from 1973 to 1974, then a departmental
councillor of Savoie, Michel Barnier was elected deputy in the 1968 legislative
elections. A promoter of environmental
protection in his department, he published a parliamentary
report in 1990 listing proposals for the environment.

He was appointed Minister of the Environment in Édouard Balladur's government in March
1993. The strengthening of the notion of sustainable development was accompanied by a
"development of competencies"; his ministry's remits did not increase, despite his
desire to promote sustainable development.

He carried the Barnier Law of 2 February 1995, which frames the competencies of the
State and local authorities in environmental matters and establishes the first
general principles of environmental law, such as the precautionary principle. He also
created the National Commission for Public Debate for major infrastructure
projects. The foundations of sustainable development were laid.

Michel Barnier called on the President of the Republic to advance international
causes such as the greenhouse effect; he thus had international treaties
signed under previous governments ratified. 26

José Rossi, Minister of Industry, Posts and Telecommunications and Foreign
Trade, 1993-1995 — José Rossi, born 18 June 1944 in Ajaccio, is a French politician.

Holder of the agrégation in public law, a Sciences Po Paris graduate, and a former member
of ministerial offices, notably those of Joseph Fontanet and Simone Veil, he became engaged
early in Corsica's political life. He held numerous mandates: mayor, departmental
councillor, member of the Corsican Assembly, and deputy. He was also Minister of
Industry, Posts and Telecommunications and Foreign Trade from October 1994 to
May 1995, during the second cohabitation, in Édouard Balladur's government.

He was successively:
- President of the General Council of Corse-du-Sud from 1985 to 1998, contributing to the
economic development of the department and in particular of the south shore of the Gulf
of Ajaccio.
- President of the Corsican Assembly from 1998 to 2004.
- President of the Corsican Economic Development Agency (2004-2006)

José Rossi always fought for recognition of Corsica's specificity within the
Republic. He was rapporteur of the bill on Corsica's status in
1991 (the Joxe law) and was one of the important actors of the "Matignon
process", which led in particular to an Equipment and Investment Plan. José Rossi
always defended the line of autonomy, considering that Corsica, if it were to remain
French, could not be managed from Paris.

Close to François Léotard, he shared the idea of federalism. He thus pushed for
the introduction of an autonomous regulatory power, a power which was not used by
the territorial collectivity of Corsica.

Because of divisions on the right, he was unable to keep his post as president after
the 2004 territorial elections and had to settle for the presidency of the Economic
Development Office.

In 2003, he supported the yes vote in the referendum on institutional reform in Corsica.

Deputy for Corse-du-Sud from 1988 to 2002. He chaired the Démocratie
libérale group in the National Assembly from 1998 to 2000. That group included 43
deputies, among them Claude Goasguen, Pascal Clément, François d'Aubert, Dominique
Bussereau, Nicolas Forissier, François Goulard, Alain Madelin, and Nicole Ameline. The
group's secretary-general was Philippe Crevel. Close to the centrists and to Alain
Madelin, José Rossi always defended liberal and social values. He wished to revive the
Indépendants, the party of which Valéry Giscard d'Estaing had been a member, in the
spirit of the 1960s and 70s.

He lost his deputy's seat in 2002, winning 42.93% of the votes cast against the
miscellaneous-left candidate Simon Renucci.

He was a candidate in the March 2008 municipal elections, again against Simon Renucci.
The list he led won 8.68% of the votes cast in the first round, after which José
Rossi rallied to the UMP list of Paul Ruault without asking to be included on it.

José Rossi was a candidate in the Corsican Territorial Assembly elections of 6 and
13 December 2015, leading the list endorsed by the Republicans and the UDI. He had
the support of the then deputy-mayor of Ajaccio, Laurent Marcangeli, and of the
deputy for Haute-Corse, Sauveur Gandolfi-Scheit. He argued that, faced with the great
regions of the Continent, Corsica must be defended and strongly represented in Paris
as in Brussels. After merging his list with that of Camille de Rocca Serra, his list
came third in the second round. He chaired the Rally Group, the first opposition
group in the Corsican Territorial Assembly chaired by Jean-Guy Talamoni.

He also chairs a think tank, "Corse prospective", which brings together Corsican
economic leaders.

Franck Borotra, Minister of Industry, Post and
Telecommunications, 1995-1997
Franck Borotra, born 30 August 1937 in Nantes (Loire-Atlantique), is a French
politician.

An engineer by training, a graduate of the French Petroleum Institute, he was notably
deputy (Rally for the Republic – RPR) for the Yvelines from 1986 to 2002.
He succeeded François Fillon as Minister of Industry, Post and
Telecommunications (November 1995-June 1997) in Alain Juppé's
government (Juppé II).
No minister succeeded him, this post having been abolished.
He was president of the Yvelines General Council from 1994 until June 2005, the date of
his resignation after 12 years at the head of the department.

Corinne Lepage, Minister of the Environment, 1996-1997

A lawyer committed to ecological causes, notably at the time of the Amoco-Cadiz
tanker shipwreck in 1978, Corinne Lepage was elected in 1989 as deputy mayor in charge
of the environment and urban planning in Cabourg. She was a candidate in the 1993
legislative elections under the banner of Génération Écologie, which she had
co-founded in 1990 with Brice Lalonde, Jean-Louis Borloo and Jean-Michel Belorgey. In
1995, she agreed to take charge of the Ministry of the Environment in Alain Juppé's
government.

The remit decree specifying the powers of the Minister of the Environment in
1995 gave Corinne Lepage responsibility for the protection of sites and outdoor
advertising, as well as, for the first time, oversight of energy, particularly
renewable energy.
She did not, however, obtain joint supervision of nuclear safety, and she lost,
compared with Ségolène Royal, the explicit mention of co-signature on decrees for major
infrastructure. Her ministry therefore remained of modest rank in the governmental
order.

Corinne Lepage promoted the notion of sustainable development in order to defend
the environment without opposing economic growth concerns. In 1996,
the Council of Ministers adopted her bill on air pollution, which makes
air quality monitoring mandatory. This bill led to Parliament's adoption of the
LAURE law (law on air and the rational use of energy) of 30
December 1996, also called the "Lepage law". This law introduced in particular
speed limits in French metropolitan areas during ozone pollution peaks.

In 1996, Corinne Lepage obtained the creation of the Prevention and
Precaution Committee, which issues opinions on public health and safety
questions. 35

Dominique Voynet, Minister of Spatial Planning and the
Environment, 1997-2001 41 Detailed inventory: https://ressources.histoire-environnement.org/Repertoire-d-archives-Cabinet-Lionel-
   Jospin-Premier-ministre-1997-2002
42 Detailed inventory: https://ressources.histoire-environnement.org/Repertoire-d-archives-Cabinet-Lionel-
   Jospin-Premier-ministre-Mission


---


Dominique Voynet entered government in 1997, after the legislative elections resulting in
cohabitation with President Jacques Chirac, in Lionel Jospin's government.
Ecologists and representatives of the various parties of the parliamentary
left sat alongside ministers from the Socialist Party. Until 2001 she was
Minister of Spatial Planning and the Environment, becoming the first
representative of the Greens in a French government.

Dominique Voynet's ministry was marked in particular by international
meetings, with the third and fourth United Nations conferences on
climate change, in 1997 and 2000.

Several laws marked Dominique Voynet's action as Minister of Spatial
Planning and the Environment: the principal one is the law of 25 June 1999 on
orientation for the planning and sustainable development of the territory, known as the
"Voynet law", which complements existing texts on decentralization, urban planning and
environmental law. Spatial planning schemes for sustainable
development were provided for there; the two areas of competence of her ministry
(Spatial Planning and Environment) were concerned in particular by the creation of
State-region plan contracts. A hunting law was prepared under her
ministry, promulgated on 26 July 2000; it gave an increased role to the National
Office for Hunting and Wildlife. Finally, she carried a bill to reform water
policy, which did not come to fruition before she left the ministry. 43

Yves Cochet, Minister of Spatial Planning and the Environment,
2001-2002

Yves Cochet, a member of several environmental defense associations and then a founding
member of the Greens party in 1984, a Rennes municipal councillor from
1989, became Minister of the Environment and Spatial Planning
in June 2001. He succeeded Dominique Voynet, who had resigned to run for the
national secretaryship of the Greens. In October 2001, he created, with the minister
in charge of agriculture, Jean Glavany, the French Agency for the
Development and Promotion of Organic Agriculture.

Roselyne Bachelot-Narquin, Minister of Ecology and Sustainable
Development, 2002-2004

Roselyne Bachelot was called into government after Jacques Chirac's victory in
2002 by Jean-Pierre Raffarin as Minister of Ecology and Sustainable Development.
A Secretary of State for Sustainable Development was attached to her, Tokia Saïfi.

Roselyne Bachelot's ministry was marked by the preparation of the Environment
Charter. Among the landmark texts, the adoption of the law on the prevention of
technological and natural risks of 30 July 2003 and of the national flood
prevention plan, as well as the national Health-Environment plan, were achievements
carried by Roselyne Bachelot's office.

On 31 March 2004, on the occasion of the reshuffle of the Raffarin government, Serge
Lepeltier succeeded Roselyne Bachelot. 49

Serge Lepeltier, Minister of Ecology and Sustainable Development, 2004-
2005

Serge Lepeltier entered Jean-Pierre Raffarin's third government on 31 March 2004,
where he succeeded Roselyne Bachelot at the Ministry of Ecology and
Sustainable Development. A Secretariat of State for sustainable development was
still placed under his authority, entrusted to Tokia Saïfi.

Serge Lepeltier's action at the Ministry of Ecology was marked by the adoption of the
Climate Plan, the inscription of the Environment Charter in the Constitution, and by
the preparation of the water law and the bear reintroduction plan. 51

Nelly Olin, Minister of Ecology and Sustainable Development, 2005-2007 — Nelly Olin was a French politician born 23 March 1941 in Paris and died in
Meulan-en-Yvelines on 26 October 2017.

A graduate of the École nationale de commerce, she began her political life in June
1995, with her election as mayor of Garges-lès-Gonesse.
Three months later, she was elected senator for Val-d'Oise, under the banner of the
Union for a Popular Movement (UMP).

She subsequently joined the governments of Jean-Pierre Raffarin and of
Dominique de Villepin successively, as minister for the fight against precarity and
exclusion from 31 March 2004 to 31 May 2005, and as Minister of Ecology and
Sustainable Development from 31 May 2005 to 15 May 2007.

As minister of ecology, she wanted France to "fully play the driving role that should be
hers at the international level". Her action would thus concern "climate
change, the disappearance of species, the transport of dangerous
substances and acid rain".

Nelly Olin also wanted the government to be "exemplary in the application of the
Kyoto protocol" and "all ministries to commit to a proactive policy on
ecology and sustainable development".

She asked citizens "to commit to global warming, the reduction of greenhouse gas
emissions, the reduction of waste production, selective
collection and sorting, the preservation of biodiversity, water quality, protection of
the coastline, the development of alternative modes of transport, noise..."

In her investiture speech the minister also recalled that waste
production was constantly increasing and wanted, on the model of the
awareness campaign "Faisons vite ça chauffe" [Let's act fast, it's heating up], an
awareness campaign on reducing waste production to be launched very quickly.

On 13 July 2007, Nelly Olin resigned from her post as first deputy mayor of the
town of Garges-lès-Gonesse and announced in October of the same year her intention
to leave French political life.

Nicole Bricq, Minister of Ecology, Sustainable Development and
Energy, 2012 — Nicole Bricq, née Vayssière, born 10 June 1947 in La Rochefoucauld (Charente) and died
6 August 2017 in Poitiers, was a French politician, member of the Socialist Party and then
of La République en marche.

A deputy and then senator, she was appointed Minister of Ecology, Sustainable
Development and Energy in the Jean-Marc Ayrault I government from May to June 2012,
then Minister of Foreign Trade in the Jean-Marc Ayrault II government, from June 2012 to
March 2014.

Not renewed in the following government, Nicole Bricq became a senator again and
joined the LREM group in 2017. She died the same year in a domestic accident.

In the early 1980s, she was appointed First Secretary of the Paris federation of the
Socialist Party. A long-time member of CERES (the current of the Socialist Party led by
Jean-Pierre Chevènement, positioned on the PS's left), then of Socialisme et République,
she was elected in 1986 as regional councillor on the Île-de-France Regional Council,
where she became chair of the Culture Commission.

From 1992 to 1993, she was technical adviser in the office of the Minister of the
Environment Ségolène Royal, who was then succeeding Brice Lalonde.

Deputy and then senator
In 1997, she was elected deputy for the sixth constituency of Seine-et-Marne (Meaux-
North). She won in the second round of a three-way race against the outgoing deputy and
(UMP) mayor of Meaux, Jean-François Copé, and the Front National candidate,
Marie-Christine Arnautu. In 1998, she produced for the Finance, General Economy and
Plan Commission an information report on environmental
taxation, suggesting in particular a reform of taxation in the service of the environment
and a strengthening of the application of the polluter-pays principle.

In 2001, she became an opposition municipal councillor in Meaux.

She failed to be re-elected in the 2002 legislative elections against Jean-François Copé,
winning in the second round barely 41% of the votes.

On 26 September 2004 she was elected senator for Seine-et-Marne, having previously
been a deputy for that same department. She sat on the Finance Commission. In October
2008, she was elected vice-chair of that Commission. In October 2011, she was re-elected
senator for Seine-et-Marne. She also became general rapporteur of the Senate Budget
within the Finance Commission, the first woman to hold this post. In that capacity, and
to prepare the Senate debate on compulsory levies, she produced in
November of that year, with the Finance Commission, a report containing a critical
and argued assessment of the compulsory levies policy for the 2007-
2012 period, together with avenues and reform proposals for the coming five-year term.
During this period, and since 2009, she was also an important supporter of François
Hollande.

On 16 May 2012, after François Hollande's election to the presidency of the Republic,
she was appointed Minister of the Environment in the first Ayrault government (she was
replaced in the Senate by Hélène Lipietz). In this task she was assisted by a
delegate minister, Frédéric Cuvillier, concerning transport and the maritime
economy. She was also in charge of energy questions.

On 13 June 2012, she announced the suspension of all exploratory hydrocarbon
drilling permits off Guyane. She wanted to protect "the marine fauna and the
environment", avoid recourse to the very polluting oil-based mud technique, and she
denounced an "unsuited and obsolete" mining code as well as the "exclusive
research permit granted to a private consortium without sufficient consideration for the
national interest".

Delphine Batho, Minister of Ecology, Sustainable Development and
Energy, 2012-2013

Delphine Batho received the full portfolio of Minister of Ecology, Sustainable
Development and Energy on 21 June 2012 in the second Ayrault
government, formed after the legislative elections. A delegate minister was attached to
her for transport.

After criticism from Delphine Batho regarding the budget allotted to her
ministry for 2014, the President of the Republic ended her duties on 2 July 2013.

Under her ministry, legal texts concerning energy were notably prepared.
One revised the mining code regarding the exploitation of conventional
hydrocarbons, and another gave the State, like EDF, the power to decide the closure of
nuclear power plants. 56

Philippe Martin, Minister of Ecology, Sustainable Development and
Energy, 2013-2014

Born in 1953 in the Hauts-de-Seine, Philippe Martin was prefect of the Gers and the
Landes before launching his political career.

A Socialist and particularly a Fabiusian, he was first elected president of the Gers
General Council in 1998, then deputy mayor of Valence-sur-Baïse in 2001, positions he
still holds today.

He became a deputy in 2002 and had his mandate renewed in 2007 and in 2012.

He is known for his fight against open-field GMO trials.


---


Philippe Martin was appointed, in Jean-Marc Ayrault's government, Minister of
Ecology, Sustainable Development and Energy from 2 July 2013 to 31 March 2014,
succeeding Delphine Batho.
A member of the team in charge of environmental questions during François
Hollande's campaign, he had the image of a man committed to environmental issues.
The new minister of ecology is above all known for his fight against
Genetically Modified Organisms (GMOs).

Three major priority dossiers were mentioned at his handover of powers: the energy
transition, which had been the subject of a national debate; the environmental
conference, planned for September 2013; and international questions.

In August 2013, he announced the forthcoming creation of a climate-energy
contribution (carbon tax).

On 2 April 2014, Ségolène Royal would succeed him on the occasion of the reshuffle of
the Valls government.

Ségolène Royal, Minister of Ecology, Sustainable Development and
Energy (2014-2016), then Minister of the Environment, Energy and the
Sea, in charge of international climate relations (2016-2017) — Ségolène Royal, born Marie Ségolène Royal on 22 September 1953 in Ouakam (Senegal),
is a French senior civil servant and politician.

She obtained a degree in economic sciences, then, after studying at the
University Centre for Political Studies (CUEP), she entered the Paris Institute of
Political Studies, from which she graduated in 1975 (Public Service section).

After a first unsuccessful attempt, she entered the National School of Administration.
During her studies, she did an internship in Martinique. She left it in 1980, 64th in her
class, which included François Hollande and Dominique de Villepin.

A member of the Socialist Party, adviser to President François Mitterrand in the
1980s, she was later a deputy for the Deux-Sèvres and a minister in the Pierre
Bérégovoy and Lionel Jospin governments.

From 3 April 1992 to 29 March 1993, she was Minister of the Environment in the
Bérégovoy government.

She had a law passed on the treatment and recycling of waste (July 1992), with support
for innovative technologies in this field. Against noise nuisances, she had a law passed
to fight noise (framework law of December 1992) covering the prevention of
transport noise, in public buildings (school canteens for example), and
workplaces. She then had a law adopted on the "reconquest of landscapes"
(January 1993). She dealt with water-related problems by working on the
implementation of the water law passed shortly before she took office, at the
initiative of her predecessor, Brice Lalonde. Her time in government was marked by
significant communication and media coverage.
Michel Barnier succeeded her in this post.

From 4 June 1997 to 27 March 2000, she was minister delegate for school education in
the Lionel Jospin government, under the Minister of National Education, Claude Allègre.

She chaired the Poitou-Charentes regional council from 2004 to 2014.

During her presidency of the Regional Council, in the field of ecology, for the
2009-2012 period, she launched, with the European Investment Bank, the "Solar
Energy Plan", with more than 400 million euros of investments. The main priorities of this
plan were the development of renewable energy production with the installation of
650,000 m² of photovoltaic panels, the strengthening of the energy autonomy of
the territories, the creation of 1,000 jobs across the regional territory, and the
reduction of greenhouse gases.

In 2009, Ségolène Royal inaugurated the Lycée Kyoto, the first school in
Europe to run exclusively on renewable energies, and not on fossil
fuels.

In 2007, Ségolène Royal was the first French woman to reach the second round
of a presidential election, but she was beaten by Nicolas Sarkozy, winning 46.9%
of the votes cast. Subsequently, she was defeated by Martine Aubry in the election
for First Secretary of the Socialist Party in 2008, then beaten again in the 2011
Socialist primary and in the 2012 legislative elections in the first
constituency of Charente-Maritime.

Ségolène Royal resigned from her mandate as president of the Poitou-Charentes
regional council on 21 April 2014, after entering the Valls government.

In the Manuel Valls and Bernard Cazeneuve governments, between 2014 and 2017, she
served as Minister of the Environment, Energy and the Sea.

Indeed, she was appointed on 2 April 2014 Minister of Ecology, Sustainable
Development and Energy. She announced her goal of having the law on the energy
transition for green growth (voted in summer 2015) passed, aiming in particular to reduce the share of
nuclear in energy production to 50% by 2025 (in line with one of
François Hollande's commitments).

Adopted under Ségolène Royal's aegis, the energy transition law of 17 August 2015
enshrines the principle of opening hydroelectric dams to competition.

François Hollande initially offered her the portfolio of Minister of
Foreign Affairs, but ultimately entrusted that function to the former Prime
Minister Jean-Marc Ayrault.

In the 11 February 2016 reshuffle, Ségolène Royal's ministry was
renamed the "Ministry of the Environment, Energy and the Sea" and was expanded to
include International Relations on Climate. She then chaired the 2015 Paris
Conference on Climate Change.

In June 2017, under the presidency of Emmanuel Macron, Ségolène Royal was appointed
ambassador in charge of international negotiation for the Arctic and
Antarctic poles. She sits on the Arctic Council and must manage the new
challenges confronting the poles.

On 24 January 2020, she was dismissed by the Council of Ministers from her functions as
ambassador for the poles.

Nicolas Hulot, Minister of Ecological and Solidarity Transition, 2017-2018
Nicolas Jacques André Hulot was born on 30 April 1955 in Lille, of the marriage of
Philippe Hulot, a gold prospector and creator of gardens, and Monique Moulun, a
manager of health centres. He has a brother, Gonzague, and a sister, Béatrice. His
paternal grandfather was an architect and his maternal great-grandfather, Georges
Moulun, was a rich industrialist who made his fortune from the construction of the
first French hydroelectric dams.

Nicolas Hulot began medical studies after his baccalaureate, which he abandoned
after 6 months. He was successively a beach attendant, sailing instructor, waiter, then
photo-reporter. From 1975 to 1980, he was hired by Gôksin Sipahioglu, founder of the
Sipa Press news agency. In 1980, he left for Guatemala, after the earthquake that
killed 20,000 people. The same year, he went on reportage to South Africa with the
navigator Éric Tabarly. In 1977, he was in Rhodesia, in the middle of the war of
independence, and interviewed Ian Smith, the Prime Minister. In 1978, after the
kidnapping of Baron Empain, he carried out surveillance, holed up for 46 days in a car
in front of his home, but was absent at the moment the baron was released by his
kidnappers and missed the hoped-for shot. On 2 November 1980, he refused to
photograph the death of Jacques Mesrine while he was among the first on the scene
of the shooting.

Nicolas Hulot began his media career at France Inter from 1978 to 1987. From
1987 to 1995, he presented the television programme Ushuaïa, le magazine de
l'extrême on TF1, co-produced with Dominique Cantier; he became one of the great
spokesmen for the protection of nature. In 1996 and 1997, he presented Opération
Okavango, then Ushuaïa Nature from 1998 to 2012. He inspired the channel Ushuaia TV,
launched on cable in March 2005, belonging to the TF1 Group. The monthly magazine
Ushuaïa Magazine was born in 2006 and lasted until 8 July 2011.

In 1990 he created the foundation which became in January 1995 the
Nicolas-Hulot Foundation for Nature and Man (recognized as being of public utility in
1996), of which he was president until June
2017.

Nicolas Hulot advised several political figures such as Laurent Fabius or
Jacques Chirac, for whom he reportedly refused in 2002 the offer to become minister
of ecology. He originated the idea of including an environment charter in the
Constitution, effective since March 2005. On 7 November 2006, he launched the
Ecological Pact to impose ecology on the French presidential campaign. He thus
wanted to challenge the candidates, mobilize citizens and feed the public debate. His
pact was signed by five of the twelve declared presidential candidates. On 13 April 2011,
Nicolas Hulot declared himself a candidate for the 2012 presidential election and
entered the 2011 ecological primaries; he lost to Eva Joly in the second round. On 6
December 2012, Nicolas Hulot was appointed "special envoy for the protection of the
planet" by the President of the Republic, François Hollande, a voluntary mission whose
"objectives are to raise awareness, inform and mobilize the international community on
the global ecological crisis and the means to face it, notably where these stakes are not
sufficiently taken into account"; his mission also included the preparation of the 21st
Climate Conference (COP21) held in Paris in 2015. In January 2013, he launched
a think tank in favour of the ecological transition. In January 2016, he left his post as
"special envoy for the protection of the planet". In February 2016, Nicolas Hulot
again refused to enter the government as minister of ecology.

On 17 May 2017, Nicolas Hulot was appointed minister of state, Minister of Ecological
and Solidarity Transition in the Édouard Philippe 1 and 2 governments, under the
presidency of Emmanuel Macron. In July 2017, Nicolas Hulot presented the
government's "Climate Plan", a bill to lead France towards carbon neutrality by
2050, in which France undertakes to cease all energy production from
coal by 2022 and to stop the sale of petrol-driven cars by 2040. To do
this, working groups per sector were initiated, procedures were simplified, and the
volume of calls for tender for photovoltaics were increased. He had a law adopted
banning the production of hydrocarbons (oil and gas) in France by 2040, with
exemptions, however. A plan to aid the energy renovation of buildings was also
presented. Also in July 2017, he authorized the culling of 40 grey wolves per
year (four more than in previous years) for a population estimated at 373
individuals on French territory. In 2017, Nicolas Hulot campaigned for the end of the
use of the glyphosate herbicide within three years. Ultimately, the European Union
renewed the herbicide's licence for five years on 27 November 2017, 18 EU member
countries having voted in favour. Nicolas Hulot continued his fight for France to exit
glyphosate within three years, but in February 2018, he
mentioned a possibility of exception for certain farmers, considering that doing
without 95% of glyphosate would be a success. On 17 January 2018, the project to
build a new airport on the territory of Notre-Dame-des-Landes, in
Loire-Atlantique, was definitively abandoned. In May 2018, parliamentarians of the
majority refused to write into law the ban on glyphosate in 2021. In March 2018, he
announced a project to reintroduce two female brown bears in the
Pyrénées-Atlantiques. On 28 August 2018, Nicolas Hulot announced his decision to
resign from the government live on France Inter's morning show. He said he had made
the decision alone, without informing either the President of the Republic or the Prime
Minister, reckoning they would try to dissuade him. He declared: "I don't want to lie to
myself any more" and motivated his departure by affirming that he had the feeling
that ecology was not a priority of the government. He was replaced on 4 September
2018 by the President of the National Assembly, François de Rugy.

Emmanuelle Wargon, Secretary of State for Ecology, 2018-2019
Emmanuelle Wargon was born on 24 February 1973 in Neuilly-sur-Seine
(Hauts-de-Seine); she is the only child of Lionel Stoléru (1937-2016), Secretary of
State from 1980 to 1981 under the presidency of Giscard d'Estaing, then from 1988 to
1991 under François Mitterrand's second presidency, and of his wife Francine Wolff, a
graduate of the ENA and administrator of the City of Paris.

She did her secondary studies at the Lycée Molière in Paris. She is a graduate of the
École des hautes études commerciales de Paris (HEC), of the Paris Institute of
Political Studies (Sciences-Po) and of the National School of Administration (ENA,
Marc Bloch class, the same class as former Prime Minister Édouard Philippe).

She began her professional career in 1997 as an auditor at the Court of
Accounts, before becoming, in 1999, a rapporteur before the Ethics Commissions.
Appointed in 2001 as technical adviser to Bernard Kouchner, minister delegate for
Health, she joined in 2002 the National Agency for the Safety of Medicines and
Health Products as deputy to the Director General.

Promoted in 2005 to referendary counsellor at the Court of Accounts, she assumed,
from 2006, the functions of director delegated to coordination and internal control
of the Assistance publique-Hôpitaux de Paris.

From 2007 to 2010, she was chief of staff to Martin Hirsch, then High Commissioner
for Active Solidarities against Poverty in the Fillon 2 government.

She was then secretary general of the social ministries (Health, Labour, Sport) until
2012, then delegate general for Professional Training and Employment at the Ministry
of Labour from 2012 to 2015.

She left the civil service in 2015 to join the Danone group as "director of
communication and public affairs" — that is to say, a lobbyist in charge of the food
group's relations with the public authorities, in charge of the agri-food group's
relations with the public authorities and of "sustainability".

On 16 October 2018, during the reshuffle of the Philippe II government, while
Emmanuelle Wargon was still employed at Danone, she was appointed Secretary of
State for Ecology under the Minister of Ecological and Solidarity Transition, François
de Rugy, succeeding Sébastien Lecornu. The latter, promoted Minister of Territorial
Collectivities, was notably in charge of energy questions.

On 14 January 2019, she was designated, with Sébastien Lecornu, to animate the
"great national debate", organized to exit the crisis engendered by the Yellow Vests
movement.

2. Strategy and general inspectorate


---


2.1 General inspectorate62
History of the producing service
The Conseil général des mines, created by imperial decree of 1810, is competent in
matters of mines, quarries, the subsoil, energy, mineral raw
materials and the industries attached to them. The Conseil général des ponts et chaussées
(CGPC), created by imperial decree of
1804, is competent in matters of roads, bridges and paved highways,
housing, railway, maritime, navigable and land routes.

The territorial inspectorate of the ministry of public works and the environment was
one of the inspection formations of the general inspectorate of public works (created in
December 1973) and then of the general inspectorate of public works and the
environment (from 1980). This formation appears as such in the various
organizational charts of the IG. Its mission was to inspect — periodically — all the
territorial services, regarding the general organization of the services, the use of
resources and personnel, and the application of ministerial directives. It was organized
into 23
districts, which were replaced in 1986 by general territorial inspection missions
(MIGT).

In 2000 a Service of the General Inspectorate of the Environment (SIGE) was created. The
CGPC and the SIGE were grouped within a new body entitled the Conseil général de
l'environnement et du développement durable (CGEDD). By decree of 9 January and 9
July 2008 the CGEDD was created.

Conseil général des mines
The Conseil général des mines (CGM) was a French administrative entity created by
imperial decree of 1810, attached to the Ministry of the Economy, Finance and
Employment. Its history and missions are closely linked to those of the Corps des mines.
The Conseil général des Mines merged in January 2009 with the Conseil général des
technologies de l'information to create the Conseil général de l'industrie, de l'énergie et
des technologies (CGIET).

On 1 March 2012, the CGIET became the Conseil général de l'économie, de l'industrie, de
l'énergie et des technologies (CGEIET). Its field of competence was broadened in
financial matters.

The Conseil général des mines (abbreviated CGM) had administrative, economic and
technical competencies, principally in the management of the
subsoil, energy, the management of risks linked to industrial activity and its products,
economic development, environmental protection, innovation and
training. It was in charge of the administrative inspection of the Regional Directorates of
Industry, Research and the Environment (DRIRE).

62 See also the archive fonds of the Conseil général des ponts et chaussées:
   http://www.archives.developpement-durable.gouv.fr/inspection-generale-r3210.html
You will find in this chapter a few archive fonds covering the 19th-20th century
period. The references come from Andrée Covol's work "Les sources de l'histoire de
l'environnement", 2003.

Presentation of the content
Besides the organizational documents of a fairly substantial and complex
apparatus, notably those of the steering committee, the fonds brings together minutes
of the various meetings in plenary session or in commissions of the National Council
of the Debate on the Energy Transition (CNDTE) and of its working groups.

The debate was moreover the occasion to organize events such as the day of 25 May
2013 and to make calls for testimonies in the form of books of
contributions. The citizen dimension is well represented by the presence of the
archives of the citizens' committee, the territorial debates and the liaison committee
of the decentralized debate, despite the notable loss of the contributions posted on
the website.

The material gathered made it possible to produce syntheses which are found in their
entirety in the fonds, and whose reading can be complemented by that of the
preparatory documents kept at least in part.

Finally, one finds there the tools for measuring the audience of the debate in the
population, statistics as well as press reviews. They constitute the testimony of the
instantaneous reception of the debate in French public opinion at the time it was held.

It should be noted that the fonds is incomplete, as the research instrument shows
several times. Important parts of the debate, such as the documents produced by
certain working groups of the CNDTE, were not transmitted, nor was a large part of
the communication apparatus. It seems relevant that the reader compare the
classification plan proposed by the producer with the one finally drawn up by the
archives from the documents received.


---


68 Detailed inventory: https://ressources.histoire-environnement.org/Repertoire-d-archives-numeriques-
   debat-national-sur-la-transition-energetique
History of the producer
The national debate on the energy transition (DNTE) was held in 2012-2013. It was,
within the framework of a policy known as environmental social dialogue, a
consultation prior to the adoption of a law on the energy transition, the future law of 18
August 2015. The debate was prepared at the environmental conference of 14 and 15
September 2012, which made it possible to draw up a roadmap for the energy transition.

Lasting about 18 months, it opened with the first meeting of a National Council of the
Debate on the Energy Transition (CNDTE), held on 29 November 2012, while the
plenary session of 18 July 2013 served as the closing. During this period, three
phases followed one another. From December 2012 to January 2013, the work consisted
of organizing the debate's calendar and gathering books of actor contributions. The
debate proper was held from February to June 2013: the citizen strand, the work of the
working groups and of the Energy Companies Contact Group (GCEE), hearings.
Finally, in July 2013, a synthesis phase made it possible to gather the lessons to be
retained from this consultation and to settle the content of the deliverables which
constitute the result of the debate communicated to the whole population.

More precisely, the activity of the DNTE, organized by an ad hoc general secretariat
directed by Thierry Wahl, was driven by a steering committee composed, besides the
minister Ms Delphine Batho, of six qualified personalities. It was the guarantor of
respect for the debate's charter. While it was held, the debate was articulated in two
strands: an institutional strand and a citizen strand.

The institutional strand was characterized by the existence of a principal body, the
National Council of the Debate on the Energy Transition (CNDTE). It brought together
the instituted actors and was organized into seven colleges gathering representatives of
several constituted bodies: parliamentarians, employee and employer unions,
non-governmental organizations (NGOs), etc. A group of experts was charged with
supporting the Council by producing the synthesis of the work of eight thematic working
groups emanating from the CNDTE and by drawing up opinions. The working groups
are presented in more detail in the part of the classification plan devoted to
their archives.

An Energy Companies Contact Group (GCEE) ensured the liaison with this
sector of the economy. Its activity appears in the DNTE fonds only
indirectly, since its own archives were not kept. The CNDTE finally organized
twenty-three hearings of personalities from very varied horizons: ministers of different
countries, academics, association activists, company heads, held in public and
broadcast on the Internet.

It should be noted that it is distinct from a permanent institution, the National Council
for the Ecological Transition (CNTE), created by the law of 27 December 2012 in order to
complement the National Council for Sustainable Development (CNDD), itself created in
2003.

On the other hand, a citizen strand aimed at directly involving the population alongside
the only organized actors and the specialists. This strand comprised three components:
conferences held at the regional level — the territorial debates — a website charged
with collecting contributions, as well as a citizen day organized on 25 May 2013. A
liaison committee of the decentralized debate (CLDD) ensured the junction between the
territorial debates and the CNDTE.

Access and use

Presentation of the content
This deposit contains largely printed, and sometimes annotated, versions of the
digital deposit made by the SGDNTE. It consists essentially of the documents of the
debate's steering committee, the sessions of the National Council and the documents
produced in the framework of the decentralized debates. A few diffuse elements
concerning the citizen day of 25 May 2013 and the citizens' committee are added. The
presence of unpublished documents relative to the digital part of the fonds should be
noted. These are documents concerning the energy days of March 2013, the energy
companies contact group and a diary of letters from elected officials and
associations. These latter elements therefore constitute, it seems, the principal
interest of the paper part of the SGDNTE fonds.

2.2 Energy management
2.2.1 Central administration
History of the producing service
Initially charged with "elaborating and implementing the government's policy in the
field of energy and raw materials", the Directorate General of Energy and Raw
Materials (DGEMP) belonged before 2008 to the Ministry of Industry
(Ministry of the Economy, Finance and Industry). At that time, it included the
service of "raw materials and the subsoil", that of "renewable energies and
rational use of energy"; and the two directorates of "hydrocarbons" and of "gas,
electricity and coal". In addition, it was charged with monitoring the activities of
the Atomic Energy Commission and its subsidiaries, for activities relating to
the production of energy and the supply of basic nuclear materials.

In 2008, it took the name of Directorate General of Energy and Climate (DGEC). Its
mission is to elaborate and implement policy relating to energy, energy
raw materials, as well as the fight against global warming and air
pollution.

It implements the measures of control and distribution of energy products and raw
materials. It ensures the proper execution of public service missions in the field of
energy. It coordinates, in consultation with associations, economic and social
partners, and with the support of all the ministries concerned, the preparation and
realization of the French programme of prevention and adaptation in matters of
climate change.

2.2.2 Environment and Energy Management Agency (ADEME)
History of the producing service
In 1990, following law no. 90-1130 of 19 December 1990 and its implementing
decree no. 91-732 of 26 July 1991, the State decided to merge the National Agency
for the Recovery and Disposal of Waste (ANRED), the Agency for Air Quality (AQA)
and the French Agency for Energy Management (AFME) and to group them into a
single agency: the Environment and Energy Management Agency (ADEME). It was
in 1992 that two major events directed ADEME's future orientations:
- the vote of the July 1992 law defining the waste management policy;
- the Rio conference, during which climatologists drew attention to the
climatic risks of the increase in carbon dioxide content in the atmosphere.

ADEME, like AFME in 1987, also continues to encourage the development of
renewable energies through two procedures: support for research and development
operations and stimulation of the market.

ADEME's resources depend on the budget granted by the government. Until
1998, the Agency also managed the product of three taxes: the parafiscal tax on
base oils, the parafiscal tax on waste, and the parafiscal tax on air
pollution. These taxes are earmarked for particular and well-defined activities. Their budgetary
management was followed, in particular, by the budget-finance service. It was an
important source of financing for the agency. 73

Association for the Development of Fuels through Wood Gasification (ASCAB)
Physical extent
9 dimabs, i.e. about 2.2 linear metres
Language of the documents
French
English
Institution responsible for intellectual access
National Archives
Access conditions
Public archives freely communicable
Mode of entry
Deposit by ADEME to the National Archives
History of the producer
Between 1980 and 1984, Creusot-Loire developed, at the Creusot Testing
Laboratory, a process for producing synthesis gas from wood. Following this
experiment, the French Agency for Energy Management (AFME), FRAMATOME
(ex Creusot-Loire) and SECOMETAL (Company for Metallurgical Studies and
Constructions) formed, in 1984, the economic interest grouping ASCAB (Association
for the Development of Fuels through Wood Gasification), whose objective was to
build and then experiment at Clamecy (Nièvre) a demonstration installation for
oxy-vapo-gasification of biomass. This installation was inaugurated on 21 November
1986.
History of the conservation
These archives were constituted and kept until today in the Paris premises of the
French Agency for Energy Management (AFME), which was one of the
members of the ASCAB economic interest grouping and which was succeeded in 1992
by the Environment and Energy Management Agency (ADEME).
Mode of classification
The fonds is classified thematically as follows:
– Art 1 to 10: Constitution, governance, bodies and correspondence of the EIG.
– Art 11 to 26: Budget, accounting and financing of the EIG.
– Art 27 to 32: Contracts and orders.
– Art 33 to 50: Communication, prospection, industrial promotion and expertise of the
process.
– Art 51 to 72: Construction and operation of the Clamecy demonstration pilot unit.
Accruals
No accrual of the fonds is to be expected, the grouping having been dissolved in the
early 1990s.
Presentation of the content
This fonds presents a certain interest for Research in the field of wood
gasification and the production of substitute fuels. Moreover, it is fairly
complete, presenting both the operation of the EIG and the expertise of the process
or the technical description of the installation (numerous plans and notices).

Regional Directorate of Industry, Research and the Environment of
Pays-de-la-Loire

Presentation of the content
This deposit concerns the energy savings certificates scheme (CEE), created by
articles 14 to 17 of law no. 2005-681 of 13 July 2005, the programme law setting the
orientations of energy policy (POPE law). It is based on an obligation to achieve energy
savings imposed by the public authorities on energy sellers called the "obligated
parties" (electricity, gas, heat, cold, domestic fuel oil and, more recently, motor
fuels). These are thereby encouraged to actively promote
energy efficiency among their customers: households, territorial
authorities or professionals.

2.2.3 National Agency for the Management of Radioactive Waste
History of the producing service
The National Agency for the Management of Radioactive Waste (ANDRA) is a
public establishment of an industrial and commercial character (ÉPIC) charged
with the management of radioactive waste in France.

It was created in November 1980 within the Atomic Energy Commission (CEA).
In July 1991, the Desgraupes report suggested that ANDRA be better associated with
the management of radioactive waste, including mining spoil (see Mining residue);
the founding text of this body (article 2 of the order of 7 November 1980) in any
case giving it competence over all long-term management operations for radioactive
waste.
In 1983, ANDRA selected twenty-eight sites whose geology would be favourable to
the installation of an underground storage centre for high activity, long-lived
radioactive waste. Strong local opposition forced ANDRA to abandon the
research on these sites.
In 1991, the Bataille law gave ANDRA its independence from the CEA, with the
status of a public establishment of an industrial and commercial character. ANDRA
is notably charged "with designing and building the new storage centres taking into
account the long-term prospects of production and management of waste, and with
carrying out all studies necessary to this end, notably the realization and operation of
underground laboratories destined for the study of deep geological
formations." ANDRA established its headquarters at Châtenay-Malabry.
In 2003, ANDRA chose to record some of its archives on permanent
paper with resistant ink, guaranteeing preservation of at least 300 years, in order to
comply with decree no. 2003-30 of 10 January 2003, which obliges the Manche
radioactive waste burial centre to perpetuate the information relating to the waste
stored beyond its closure.
In the framework of the dismantling of the French nuclear power plants, ANDRA
launched a call for tenders in September 2015 with the aim of developing the most
innovative technological solutions seeking to minimize the impact in terms of nuclear
waste. According to estimates, the dismantling of the plants will generate 2.1 million cubic
metres of waste. The Constitutional Council struck down on 5 August 2015 the
insertion into the law for growth, activity and equality of economic opportunity,
known as the Macron law, of an article defining reversibility adopted by the Senate
on the preceding 18 April on the proposal of Gérard Longuet, on the grounds that it
presented no link, even indirect, with the provisions appearing in the bill. This
provision was nevertheless taken up in the law setting the framework of the Cigéo
project13 adopted in July 2016.
For the 2017 finance bill, ANDRA's budget is charged to budget programme
174 Energy, Climate and After-mines of the ministry of ecology. 151 151 Sources: Wikipedia

2.4 Europe and international161
History of the producing service
The General Secretariat (SG) is charged with assisting the ministers162 in the
administration of
the ministry (financial function, human resources, supervision of national public
establishments, internal and external communication strategy, legal questions,
elaboration of the minister's position internationally and in Europe, defence and security
policy within the ministry's perimeter). This service results from the merger of the former
general secretariat of the ministry of public works, of the General Directorate of
administration and
development (DGAD), which became the General Directorate of
administration, finance and international affairs of the ministry of the
environment.

The former General Directorate of Industry and Raw Materials was in charge of
international relations
